And it's especially serious because we're talking about the most populated city in Galicia and, on the other hand, about the most important urban conurbation in the northwest, which at the same time are two cities, two nodes, fundamental to the Atlantic axis. It is absolutely abnormal, in addition, within the European context that two large cities that are part of a single urban system because they do not have a rail mobility option, because it is clear that what is not a real option today. But we also have a background structural problem, which is that the line from The Coruña to the Port is formed by two separate systems: from the Coruña to Vigo we have a railway system that ends at the Urzáiz station; and from Vigo to the Port we have another system from the Guixar station. I mean, in Vigo you clearly see that structural problem with two stations for two segregated systems. So in this non-law proposal we raised the need to solve the problem in two directions: on the one hand, the short-term strategy to solve the need to halt the dismantling process; and on the other hand, the medium- and long-term strategy to solve the underlying structural problem. In the short-term objective — as I say — there must be a halt to this covert dismantling process that has been systematically fostered by the Central Government for years. It is urgent to improve the rolling stock, it is urgent to increase frequencies — and here I want to point out that we present a self-amendment because when we present this initiative the context of the pandemic was another, and we believe that the level of frequencies is also normal to adapt to the situation of the pandemic. And we have to move forward in electrification. But we also know that all of this is still patchy and that the fundamental thing is the medium-term objective we have -- in a period of about ten years -- that is to solve the southern exit. This is the fundamental work, the key to the vault to make it a single continuous Atlantic axis from The Coruña to Lisbon. Well, the situation I've described is serious, it's abnormal, but it's not random. It is the result of a state project that was based mainly on the development of a radial model of infrastructure that it had and has as its fundamental objective to convert Madrid into political capital, economic capital, and now financial capital, the cost of the rest of the territories. And this has been the case in Spain since the 18th century. This radial infrastructure model has never been developed under criteria of social profitability, not even under liberal criteria of empowerment of economic productivity; but always under the unique criterion of spatially ordering political power to the service of capital. And, of course, in the case of Madrid this has always been quite problematic, because Madrid has always been a capital against nature, it is an exception throughout the European context. There is no other capital that has no maritime-fluvial connection. And that, in the face of a peninsula context full of harbor cities with geo-strategic compositions in the Atlantic and in the Mediterranean much better, I had a great effort to build that anti-natural capital. Converting Madrid to this large capital required an effort that was channelled mainly into two strategies: on the one hand, the design of the radial model of infrastructures, and, on the other hand, the strategy of the disarticulation of coastal corridors. And this is the bottom line. It's not like Galicia's trains are late because they are. There's a tension between two different models of understanding the territorial vertebration of the peninsula, and there are two different models of understanding what the geopolitical role Galicia should play. For the Galician State it must simply be an extreme of that radial structure subject to the project of accumulation and concentration of capital, and also of human capital, towards Madrid — and at the same time, we must boycott that coastal corridor. And from our perspective this is unacceptable and unacceptable from our daily mobility needs, from our economic development potential, from the potential that we have to be part of one of the most powerful urban systems in the Peninsula and the European context. Because, despite this radial model that was intended to be imposed on the Peninsula, there are currently three major urban systems: Madrid, the Mediterranean coastal system and the Atlantic coastal system. And as much as the state has tried to avoid it, we are a continuous urban system from The Coruña to Lisbon. We are despite centuries of boycott programmed; we are despite dictatorships that have strengthened borders, maps that systematically erase Portugal, which erase our territory to the south, because coastal dynamics are more powerful; we are because coastal dynamics are more powerful, because cultural continuity, continuity of forms of occupation of the territory is very strong; and, despite Madrid, we are a single Atlantic urban system, which only in the northern region of Portugal and Galicia is 6.5 million inhabitants. [[Applause]] And besides, an urban system that in the case of Galicia has a series of specifics by which the radial model poses a great danger and the coastal corridor would bring great advantages. Our urban system is a polycentric model of medium cities; that is, we don't have a great city as is the case of Barcelona that can counter the suction dynamics that generates the radial model. That's why the radial model in our case is a great danger. On the contrary, we have a model that works from the complementarity of the different cities, the proximity relationships and the articulation of a continuous urban that goes to Porto and extends to Lisbon. That's why the coastal corridor would be a great opportunity to connect the main nodes in thirty-minute intervals or reaching up to an hour, with the goal of placing Vigo-Braga in thirty-five minutes and Vigo-O Port in less than an hour. Because the bottom line is to strengthen the urban polycentric system we have on the northwest of the Peninsula. And the railway connection to the south of Vigo must also be understood as an opportunity to improve infrastructure throughout the metropolitan area to the south, it is an opportunity to connect Vigo to the whole area and reorder all communications. This southern exit plan towards Vigo and the Vigo port connection must go hand in hand to a study of what is the whole neighbourhood system, because we have the potential to recycle the current infrastructure to define a new neighbourhood system south of the city. It must also be an opportunity to improve the conflicting places of the infrastructure, such as the passage through the village of Porriño, which divides the city into two. You have to think about it from the point of view of the set, not just from the point of view of the large backend. And we have been under the role that Madrid imposes on us for too long, and we all know that both the People's Party and the Socialist Party in Moncloa have always ruled in the service of this radial model; besides, with some opacity, because these types of projects have never been openly discussed, they have always been discussed in offices or in camilla tables with a political entrepreneurship that exercises power and sovereignty behind citizenship. With this initiative, therefore, we also want to give light here, from the German Parliament, to demand a correction of that policy. We want to send a clear message with this initiative: we are a single urban system and therefore we want a single rail system, continuous, articulated, emancipated from centralist politics, so that we can play a fundamental role in the Atlantic and in the international context. And, in addition, this is a fundamental moment, because in recent decades, in the context of the European negotiations on large international infrastructures, it has actually given a division of geopolitical roles as to what role each part of the peninsula should play. There is a kind of tacit agreement within the European context that Portugal has developed the ports of the Atlantic façade and Spain would focus on the ports of the Mediterranean and the Basque Country. You just have to see all the infrastructure investments in railway connections of railway intermodality with ports. And with European funds in recent decades Spain has decided to bet solely on the Atlantic on the Madrid-Lisboa connection, because the fundamental objective, once again, was to place Madrid as the fundamental node of the connections between the Portuguese ports of the Atlantic and Europe. But the Portuguese Government has just made it clear that this is no longer its model, that they will not bet on that model in passengers, that its priority is no longer Lisbon-Madrid and will be the development of the Atlantic axis. From there too our self-amendment. After these statements, we have created a need to update our initiative on the progress being made on the Portuguese side. That's why we're at a fundamental moment, we have the hand held on the Portuguese side, it's crucial that the southern part of the Atlantic axis is looking north and we also have an injection of European funds for the next few years...
